A_{1},A_{2}\subseteq[s]^{n}
be the subset of points where
f_{i}
agrees with
Q_{j_{1}}
and
Q_{j_{2}}
respectively. Let
A\subseteq[s]^{n}
be the subset of points where
f_{i+1}
agrees with
P
. From the proof of Item 2, we have that
A_{1},A_{2}\subseteq A
. Since two distinct
d
-junta-sums cannot agree on more than
1-1/s^{d}
fraction of inputs (
2.6
), we have
|A_{1}\cap A_{2}|\leq(1-1/s^{d})s^{n}
. Hence,
|A|\geq|A_{1}\cup A_{2}|=|A_{1}|+|A_{2}|-|A_{1}\cap A_{2}|\geq s^{n}\left(2-\delta(f_{i},Q_{j_{1}})-\delta(f_{i},Q_{j_{2}})-1+1/s^{d})\right)
. Since
|A|=s^{n}\left(1-\delta(f_{i+1},P)\right)
, we get
\delta(f_{i+1},P)\leq\delta(f_{i},Q_{j_{1}})+\delta(f_{i},Q_{j_{2}})-1/s^{d}
, or equivalently
\rho(u)\geq\rho(v_{j_{1}})+\rho(v_{j_{2}})
.
∎

Having reduced the problem to showing combinatorial bound over
\mathbb{F}_{q}
, which we state below and prove in the next subsection.

Theorem D.9
.

For every
\varepsilon>0
, finite field
\mathbb{F}_{q}
and subset
S\subseteq\mathbb{F}_{q}
of size
s\geq 2
, the family
\mathcal{J}_{d}(S^{n},\mathbb{F}_{q})
is
(1/s^{d}-\varepsilon,O_{q,\varepsilon}(1))
-list-decodable.

With the above theorem, we can now finish the proof of the combinatorial bound for
p
-primary groups.

Proof of
Theorem

D.2
.

The proof follows by combining
Lemma

D.7
,
Lemma

D.6
and
Theorem

D.9
.
∎

D.2.2
Combinatorial Bound for
\mathbb{F}_{q}

Throughout this subsection, we fix a finite field
\mathbb{F}_{q}
and a subset
S\subseteq\mathbb{F}_{q}
of size
s
arbitrarily. We think of
q\geq 2,s\geq 2
and
d\geq 1
as constants. Furthermore, we fix a monomial ordering (denoted
\preceq
) over the monomials to be the
graded lexicographic order
(see
[
ABPSS25
]
for a definition) and denote by
\textrm{LM}(P)
the leading monomial of a polynomial
P
(assuming it is non-zero). We will use the notation
m_{1}\succeq m_{2}
to mean that
m_{2}\preceq m_{1}
and
m_{1}\succneq m_{2}
to mean that
m_{2}\preceq m_{1}
and
m_{1}\neq m_{2}
.

We show the following lemma which effectively reduces the list-decoding problem to bounding the number of polynomials in the list with pairwise distinct monomials.

Lemma D.10

(
Distinct leading monomials).

If
P_{1},\dots,P_{t}\in\mathcal{J}_{d}(S^{n},\mathbb{F}_{q})
are such that
\delta(f,P_{i})\leq 1/s^{d}-\varepsilon
for all
i\in[t]
and some
f:S^{n}\to\mathbb{F}_{q}
, then there exists a function
f^{\prime}:S^{n}\to\mathbb{F}_{q}
such that there are at least
\ell\geq\Omega({\log_{q}t})
many polynomials
Q_{1},\dots,Q_{\ell}\in\mathcal{J}_{d}(S^{n},\mathbb{F}_{q})
such that
\delta(f^{\prime},Q_{i})\leq 1/s^{d}-\varepsilon
and
\textrm{LM}(Q_{i})
are pairwise distinct for
i\in[\ell]
.

Then, we prove the following “tail bound” for polynomials with
pairwise disjoint
leading monomials.

Lemma D.11

(
Tail bound for disjoint leading monomials).

Let
P_{1},\dots,P_{t}\in\mathcal{J}_{d}(S^{n},\mathbb{F}_{q})
be such that
\textrm{LM}(P_{i})
are pairwise disjoint for
i\in[t]
. Then:

\Pr_{{\bf a}\sim S^{n}}\bigg[\bigg|\{i\in[t]:P_{i}({\bf a})=0\}\bigg|\geq(1-1/s^{d}+\eta)t\bigg]\leq\exp(-\Omega(\eta^{2}t)).

With the above lemmas in place, we are ready to finish the proof of the main result of this subsection i.e.,
Theorem

D.9
.

Proof of
Theorem

D.9
.

Using
Lemma

D.10
, it suffices to show that if
Q_{1},\dots,Q_{\ell}\in\mathcal{J}_{d}(S^{n},\mathbb{F}_{q})
are such that
\mathrm{LM}(Q_{i})
are pairwise distinct for
i\in[\ell]
and
\delta(Q_{i},f)\leq 1/s^{d}-\varepsilon
for some
f:S^{n}\to\mathbb{F}_{q}
, then that
\ell=O_{q,\varepsilon}(1)
. Applying the sunflower lemma (see e.g.
[
ER60
]
) for the multisets determined by the leading monomials of
Q_{i}
’s, we can find a subset of indices
i_{1},\dots,i_{\ell^{\prime}}
for some
\ell^{\prime}\geq\Omega_{d}(\ell^{1/d})
such that
\mathrm{LM}(Q_{i_{j}})
form a sunflower for
j\in[\ell^{\prime}]
. That is, there exists variables
\{x_{j}:j\in C\}
and
\{e_{j}\in\mathbb{Z}_{s}:j\in C\}
where
C\in{[n]\choose\leq d}
such that
\mathrm{LM}(Q_{i_{j}})=\prod_{j^{\prime}\in C}x_{j^{\prime}}^{e_{j^{\prime}}}\cdot m_{j}
and
m_{j}
are monomials over the variables indexed by
[n]\setminus C
and are pairwise disjoint for
j\in[\ell^{\prime}]
. Without loss of generality, we will assume that
i_{j}=j
for all
j\in[\ell^{\prime}]
. We will now express each
Q_{i}
for
i\in[\ell^{\prime}]
(uniquely) as follows:

Q_{i}(\mathbf{x})=\prod_{j\in C}x_{j}^{e_{j}}\cdot Q_{i}^{(1)}(\mathbf{x}_{[n]\setminus C})+Q_{i}^{(2)}(\mathbf{x}),

where
Q_{i}^{(1)}
is a polynomial over variables indexed by
[n]\setminus C
and
Q_{i}^{(2)}
does not contain any monomial dividing
\prod_{j\in C}x_{j}^{e_{j}}
. We note that since the leading monomial of
Q_{i}
is
\prod_{j\in C}x_{j}^{e_{j}}\cdot m_{i}
, by our definition of monomial ordering, we must have that
\mathrm{LM}(Q_{i}^{(1)})=m_{i}
. Let
{\bf a}\sim S^{n}
be sampled by first choosing
{\bf a}^{\prime}\sim S^{[n]\setminus C}
uniformly at random and then
{\bf a}^{\prime\prime}\sim S^{C}
uniformly and independently. Letting
d^{\prime}=d-|C|
, we may now apply the tail bound (
Lemma

D.11
) to
Q_{i}^{(1)}
to get that

\Pr_{{\bf a}^{\prime}}\bigg[\bigg|\{i\in[t]:Q_{i}^{(1)}({\bf a}^{\prime})=0\}\bigg|\geq(1-1/s^{d^{\prime}}+\varepsilon/2)\ell^{\prime}\bigg]\leq\exp(-\Omega(\varepsilon^{2}\ell^{\prime})).

In fact, by applying it to
Q_{i}^{(1)}-\alpha
for
\alpha\in\mathbb{F}_{q}
and by a union bound, we get that

\displaystyle\Pr_{{\bf a}^{\prime}}\bigg[\exists\alpha\in\mathbb{F}_{q}\text{~such that~}\bigg|\{i\in[t]:Q_{i}^{(1)}({\bf a}^{\prime})=\alpha\}\bigg|\geq(1-1/s^{d^{\prime}}+\varepsilon/2)\ell^{\prime}\bigg]\leq q\cdot\exp(-\Omega(\varepsilon^{2}\ell^{\prime})).

(43)

Now, let us use the notation

Q_{i}^{\prime}({\mathbf{x}}_{C})=Q_{i}(\mathbf{x}_{C},{\bf a}^{\prime})

to denote the corresponding restricted functions obtained by setting the variables in
{[n]\setminus C}
to
{\bf a}^{\prime}
. Similarly, let
f^{\prime}:S^{C}\to\mathbb{F}_{q}
be the restricted function
f^{\prime}(\mathbf{x}_{C})=f(\mathbf{x}_{C},{\bf a}^{\prime})
. For a uniformly random choice of
{\bf a}^{\prime}
, let
{\mathcal{B}}
denote the “bad” event that the
multiset
of functions
\{Q_{i}^{\prime}:i\in[\ell^{\prime}]\}
has a function occurring at least
(1-1/s^{d^{\prime}}+\varepsilon/2)\ell^{\prime}
many times. The bound from
Equation

43
immediately implies that

\displaystyle\Pr_{{\bf a}^{\prime}}[\mathcal{B}]\leq q\cdot\exp(-\Omega(\varepsilon^{2}\ell^{\prime})).

(44)

Conditioned on
\mathcal{B}
not occurring, we note that there are at least
(1/s^{d^{\prime}}-\varepsilon/2)\ell^{\prime}
indices
i\in[\ell^{\prime}]
such that
Q_{i}^{\prime}\neq f^{\prime}
as functions over
S^{C}
. More formally,

\Pr_{i\sim[\ell^{\prime}]}\bigg[Q_{i}^{\prime}\neq f^{\prime}~\mid~\overline{\mathcal{B}}\bigg]\geq 1/s^{d^{\prime}}-\varepsilon/2.

Since two different functions over
|C|
variables must differ on a random input with probability at least
1/s^{|C|}
, we further get:

\displaystyle\Pr_{i\sim[\ell^{\prime}],{\bf a}^{\prime\prime}}\bigg[Q_{i}^{\prime}({\bf a}^{\prime\prime})\neq f^{\prime}({\bf a}^{\prime\prime})~\mid~\overline{\mathcal{B}}\bigg]\geq\left(\frac{1}{s^{d^{\prime}}}-\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\right)\frac{1}{s^{|C|}}\geq\frac{1}{s^{d}}-\frac{\varepsilon}{2}.

(45)

Combining (
44
) and (
45
), we obtain

\Pr_{i\sim[\ell^{\prime}],{\bf a}\sim S^{n}}\bigg[Q_{i}({\bf a})\neq f({\bf a})\bigg]\geq\frac{1}{s^{d}}-\frac{\varepsilon}{2}-\frac{q}{2^{\Omega(\varepsilon^{2}\ell^{\prime})}}.

However, we note that since
\delta(Q_{i},f)\leq 1/s^{d}-\varepsilon
for all
i\in[\ell^{\prime}]
, the left hand side of the above inequality must be at most
\frac{1}{s^{d}}-\varepsilon
. Put together, they give the required bound of
\ell^{\prime}=O_{q,\varepsilon}(1)
, and thus
\ell=O_{q,\varepsilon}(1)
.
∎

We now give the proofs of the above two lemmas. First, we start with the reduction to counting polynomials with distinct leading monomials, i.e.,
Lemma

D.10
.

Proof of
Lemma

D.10
.

Let
\ell
be an integer such that
t\in[q^{\ell},q^{\ell+1})
(so we have
\ell\geq\Omega(\log_{q}t)
). We will prove the following inductive claim. We recall that
L_{\varepsilon}(f)
denotes the set of polynomials in
\mathcal{J}_{d}(S^{n},\mathbb{F}_{q})
that are at distance at most
1/s^{d}-\varepsilon
from the function
f
.

Inductive claim.
For every
0\leq i\leq\ell
, there exists a function
f_{i}:S^{n}\to\mathbb{F}_{q}
, polynomials
Q_{1},Q_{2},\dots,Q_{i}\in\mathcal{J}_{d}(S^{n},\mathbb{F}_{q})
, and a set of polynomials
\mathcal{Q}_{i}\subseteq\mathcal{J}_{d}(S^{n},\mathbb{F}_{q})
such that:

•

Q_{1},\dots,Q_{i}\in L_{\varepsilon}(f_{i})
and
Q\in L_{\varepsilon}(f_{i})
for all
Q\in\mathcal{Q}_{i}
,

•

\mathrm{LM}(Q_{1})\succneq\mathrm{LM}(Q_{2})\succneq\dots\succneq\mathrm{LM}(Q_{i})\succneq\mathrm{LM}(Q)
for all
Q\in\mathcal{Q}_{i}
, and

•

|\mathcal{Q}_{i}|\geq q^{\ell-i}
.

We note that the base case
i=0
is true with
f_{0}=f
and
\mathcal{Q}_{0}=\{P_{1},P_{2},\dots,P_{t}\}
. And proving the inductive claim for
i=\ell
finishes the proof of
Lemma

D.10
. We now assume the inductive claim holds for a fixed
i<\ell
and prove it for
i+1
.

Let
P\in\mathcal{J}_{d}(S^{n},\mathbb{F}_{q})
be the “plurality polynomial” of
\mathcal{Q}_{i}
, i.e., we determine each coefficient of
P
by taking a plurality vote of the corresponding coefficients from the polynomials in
\mathcal{Q}_{i}
(by breaking ties arbitrarily). We then define
f_{i+1}:S^{n}\to\mathbb{F}_{q}
to be

f_{i+1}=f_{i}-P.

We now define
Q_{1}^{\prime},\dots,Q_{i}^{\prime},Q^{\prime}_{i+1}\in\mathcal{J}_{d}(S^{n},\mathbb{F}_{q})
and
\mathcal{Q}_{i+1}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{J}_{d}(S^{n},\mathbb{F}_{q})
such that the three items of the inductive claim hold for them. We let
\mathcal{Q}_{i}^{\prime}=\{Q-P:Q\in\mathcal{Q}_{i}\}
and set

Q_{j}^{\prime}=Q_{j}-P
for
j\in[i]
. We now set
Q_{i+1}^{\prime}
to be a polynomial from
\mathcal{Q}_{i}^{\prime}
with the greatest leading monomial (ignoring the zero polynomial if it exists and breaking ties arbitrarily). Then we set
\mathcal{Q}_{i+1}^{\prime}
to be the subset of polynomials in
\mathcal{Q}_{i}^{\prime}
with leading monomial strictly smaller than that of
Q^{\prime}_{i+1}
, i.e.:

\mathcal{Q}_{i+1}^{\prime}=\{Q^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Q}_{i}^{\prime}:\mathrm{LM}(Q^{\prime})\precneq\mathrm{LM}(Q^{\prime}_{i+1})\}.

It remains to prove that the three conditions of the inductive claim actually hold for the above definitions.

•

We have that
\delta(Q_{j}^{\prime},f_{i+1})=\delta(Q_{j}-P,f_{i}-P)=\delta(Q_{j},f_{i})\leq 1/s^{d}-\varepsilon
, therefore
Q_{j}^{\prime}\in L_{\varepsilon}(f_{i+1})
for all
j\in[i]
. Similarly, we have
Q_{i+1}^{\prime}\in L_{\varepsilon}(f_{i+1})
and
Q^{\prime}\in L_{\varepsilon}(f_{i+1})
for all
Q^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}_{i+1}
.

•

We note that
\mathrm{LM}(P)\precneq\mathrm{LM}(Q_{i})
since the coefficients of all monomials
m\succeq\mathrm{LM}(Q_{i})
in all
Q\in\mathcal{Q}_{i}^{\prime}
(and hence in
P
) are zero by the induction hypothesis. Therefore,
\mathrm{LM}(Q_{j}^{\prime})=\mathrm{LM}(Q_{j})
for
j\in[i]
and we have
\mathrm{LM}(Q_{1}^{\prime})\succneq\mathrm{LM}(Q_{2}^{\prime})\succneq\dots\succneq\mathrm{LM}(Q_{i}^{\prime})
. It also follows that
\mathrm{LM}(Q_{i}^{\prime})\succneq\mathrm{LM}(Q_{i+1}^{\prime})
and
\mathrm{LM}(Q_{i+1}^{\prime})\succneq\mathrm{LM}(Q)
for all
Q\in\mathcal{Q}_{i+1}^{\prime}
by the definitions of
Q^{\prime}_{i+1}
and
\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}_{i+1}
.

•

We have that
|\mathcal{Q}_{i}^{\prime}|=|\mathcal{Q}_{i}|\geq q^{\ell-i}
by induction hypothesis. By the definition of
Q_{i+1}^{\prime}
, we observe that
\mathrm{LM}(Q^{\prime})\preceq\mathrm{LM}(Q_{i+1}^{\prime})
for all
Q^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Q}_{i}^{\prime}
and we will show that at least
1/q
fraction of
Q^{\prime}
’s have leading monomial
strictly
smaller than that of
Q_{i+1}^{\prime}
. By the nature of the construction of
P
using the plurality vote, we observe that at least
1/q
fraction of the polynomials
Q\in\mathcal{Q}_{i}^{\prime}
agree with
P
on the coefficient
\mathrm{LM}(Q_{i+1}^{\prime})
. The corresponding polynomials
Q^{\prime}=Q-P
have coefficient of
\mathrm{LM}(Q_{i+1})
as zero. In other words there are at least
|\mathcal{Q}_{i}|/q
polynomials
Q^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Q}_{i}^{\prime}
with leading coefficient strictly smaller than
\mathrm{LM}(Q_{i+1}^{\prime})
, and hence by our definition of
\mathcal{Q}_{i+1}^{\prime}
, it must be of size
|\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}_{i+1}|\geq|\mathcal{Q}_{i}^{\prime}|/q\geq q^{\ell-{(i+1)}}
.

This finishes the proof of the inductive claim.
∎

We now prove the tail bound for polynomials with pairwise disjoint leading monomials (
Lemma

D.11
).

Proof of
Lemma

D.11
.

We will use the following theorem of Panconesi and Srinivasan
[
PS97
]
which reduces the task of showing tail bounds to proving a certain “independence” relation among the events.

Theorem D.12

(
[
PS97
]
Theorem 3.4)
.

Let
Z_{1},\dots,Z_{t}
be Boolean random variables and
\alpha\in[0,1]
be such that for every subset
S\subseteq[t]
, we have that
\Pr[\bigwedge_{i\in S}Z_{i}=1]\leq\alpha^{|S|}
. Then, for every
\eta>0
, we have

\Pr\bigg[\sum_{i\in[t]}Z_{i}\geq(\alpha+\eta)t\bigg]\leq\exp(-\Omega(\eta^{2}t)).

Because of the above theorem, it suffices to show the following: for every
t\in\mathbb{N}
and non-zero polynomials
P_{1},\dots,P_{t}\in\mathcal{J}_{d}(S^{n},\mathbb{F}_{q})
for which
\mathrm{LM}(P_{i})
are pairwise disjoint for
i\in[t]
, that:

\displaystyle\Pr_{{\bf a}\sim S^{n}}\bigg[P_{i}({\bf a})=0~\text{~for all~}i\in[t]\bigg]\leq\left(1-\frac{1}{s^{d}}\right)^{t}.

(46)

The proof follows the same
footprint bound
technique as used in
[
ABPSS25
]
. In particular, letting

Z=\{{\bf a}\in S^{n}:P_{i}({\bf a})=0\text{~for all~}i\in[t]\}

denote the set of common zeroes, we will prove an upper bound on the dimension of all functions from
Z
to
\mathbb{F}_{q}
i.e.,
|Z|
. Let
f:Z\to\mathbb{F}_{q}
be an arbitrary function. We will show that it can be expressed as a polynomial of individual degree at most
s-1
over
\mathbb{F}_{q}
, without using any monomial divisible by any of the
\mathrm{LM}(P_{i})
for
i\in[t]
. That is, these are the monomials
{\bf x}^{{\bf e}}
for
{\bf e}\in E
, where
E\subseteq\mathbb{Z}_{s}^{n}
is defined below, and
\mathrm{LM}(P_{i})={\bf x}^{{\bf m}_{i}}
for some
{\bf m}_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{s}^{n}
16
16

16

Here we treat
\mathbb{Z}_{s}^{n}=\{0,1,\dots,s-1\}^{n}
as a subset of
\mathbb{Z}^{n}
and define the monomial
{\mathbf{x}}^{{\bf m}}=\prod_{i\in[n]}x_{i}^{m_{i}}
.
:

E=\{{\bf e}\in\mathbb{Z}_{s}^{n}:\forall i\in[t]~\exists j\in[n]~e_{j}<m_{i,j}\}.

Using the fact that the supports of
{\bf m}_{i}
are pairwise disjoint (over
i\in[t]
) and are of size at most
d
, we get that
|E|\leq\left(1-\frac{1}{s^{d}}\right)^{t}\cdot s^{n}
.
We will show that there exists field elements
(c_{{\bf e}})_{{\bf e}\in E}
such that
f({\bf x})=\sum_{{\bf e}\in E}c_{{\bf e}}\cdot{\bf x}^{{\bf e}}
for all
{\bf x}\in Z
. This shall finish the proof of (
46
) and thus
Lemma

D.11
as it shows that
|Z|\leq|E|\leq\left(1-\frac{1}{s^{d}}\right)^{t}\cdot s^{n}
. Hence, it remains to prove that
f
can be expressed as a linear combination of monomials in
E
. Since
Z\subseteq S^{n}
, we know that there exists a polynomial representation for
f
of individual degree at most
s-1
: suppose
Q(\mathbf{x})=\sum_{{\bf e}\in\mathbb{Z}_{s}^{n}}c_{{\bf e}}\cdot{\mathbf{x}}^{{\bf e}}
for some
c_{{\bf e}}\in\mathbb{F}_{q}
is such that
f({\bf x})=Q(\mathbf{x})
for all
\mathbf{x}\in Z
. If
c_{{\bf e}}=0
for all
{\bf e}\notin E
, we are done. Otherwise, there exists an
i\in[t]
such that
\mathrm{LM}(P_{i})
divides
{\bf x}^{{\bf e}^{\prime}}
for some
{\bf e}^{\prime}
such that
c_{{\bf e}^{\prime}}\neq 0
(say that
\mathbf{x}^{{\bf e}^{\prime}}=\mathrm{LM}(P_{i})\cdot\mathbf{x}^{{\bf e}^{\prime\prime}}
); w.l.o.g. let
{\mathbf{x}}^{{\bf e}^{\prime}}
be the largest monomial in the monomial ordering such that this holds. Then, we note that we can replace the monomial
\mathbf{x}^{{\bf e}^{\prime}}
with the polynomial
\mathbf{x}^{{\bf e}^{\prime\prime}}(\mathrm{LM}(P_{i})-P_{i}/c)
in the polynomial
\sum_{{\bf e}\in\mathbb{Z}_{s}^{n}}c_{{\bf e}}\cdot{\mathbf{x}}^{\bf e}
while still computing
f
, where
c\in\mathbb{F}_{q}^{\times}
is the coefficient of
\mathrm{LM}(P_{i})
in
P_{i}
. This is due to the fact that
P_{i}
(and therefore
P_{i}/c
) evaluates to
0
over
Z
. Let
Q^{\prime}
denote the polynomial obtained by such a transformation. We claim that
\mathrm{LM}(Q^{\prime})\precneq\mathrm{LM}(Q)
. This is because all the new non-zero monomials introduced by the transformation are of the form
\mathbf{x}^{{\bf e}^{\prime\prime}}\cdot\mathbf{x}^{{\bf e}^{\prime\prime\prime}}
for some
\mathbf{x}^{{\bf e}^{\prime\prime\prime}}\precneq\mathrm{LM}(P_{i})
, and so
\mathbf{x}^{{\bf e}^{\prime\prime}}\cdot\mathbf{x}^{{\bf e}^{\prime\prime\prime}}\precneq\mathbf{x}^{{\bf e}^{\prime\prime}}\cdot\mathrm{LM}(P_{i})=\mathbf{x}^{{\bf e}^{\prime}}=\mathrm{LM}(Q)
using the monomial ordering property. Hence,
\mathrm{LM}(Q^{\prime})\precneq\mathrm{LM}(Q)
. While the leading monomial of the polynomial computing has decreased, it may be possible that
Q^{\prime}
contains monomials with individual degree at least
s
. We now argue that we can design a new polynomial
Q^{\prime\prime}
such that
\mathrm{LM}(Q^{\prime\prime})\preceq\mathrm{LM}(Q^{\prime})
and
Q^{\prime\prime}(\mathbf{x})=Q^{\prime}(\mathbf{x})=f(\mathbf{x})
for all
\mathbf{x}\in Z
. The idea is to use the equation
\prod_{a\in S}(x_{i}-a)=0
to replace the powers of the variable
x_{i}
greater than
s-1
with smaller powers — this only results in monomials that are smaller in the monomial order. Thus, by repeating the above two steps for a finite number of times, we will have a polynomial representing
f
only using monomials from
E
.
∎
